Primal-Dual Policy Optimization for Linear CMDPs with Adversarial Losses
Kihyun Yu, Seoungbin Bae, Dabeen Lee

Abstract
Existing work on linear constrained Markov decision processes (CMDPs) has primarily focused on stochastic settings, where the losses and costs are either fixed or drawn from fixed distributions. However, such formulations are inherently vulnerable to adversarially changing environments. To overcome this limitation, we propose a primal-dual policy optimization algorithm for online finite-horizon adversarial linear CMDPs, where the losses are adversarially chosen under full-information feedback and the costs are stochastic under bandit feedback. Our algorithm is the first to achieve sublinear regret and constraint violation bounds in this setting, both bounded by 𝒪 ~ ​ ( K 3 / 4 ) \widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28723\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 84054785 , where K \mathchar 29003 denotes the number of episodes. The algorithm introduces and runs with a new class of policies, which we call weighted LogSumExp softmax policies, designed to adapt to adversarially chosen loss functions. Our main result stems from the following key contributions: (i) a new covering number argument for the weighted LogSumExp softmax policies, and (ii) two novel algorithmic components—periodic policy mixing and a regularized dual update—which allow us to effectively control both the covering number and the dual variable. We also report numerical results that validate our theoretical findings on the performance of the algorithm.



1
Introduction

Safe reinforcement learning (RL) studies sequential decision-making under safety constraints through interaction with an unknown environment. Many real-world applications have been explored under the safe RL framework, including autonomous driving
(
20
)
, robotics
(
2
)
, and healthcare
(
10
)
. A common modeling framework for safe RL is the online constrained Markov decision process (CMDP) formulation, where the agent seeks a policy that minimizes (or maximizes) cumulative expected loss (or reward), while ensuring that the expected cumulative cost does not exceed a given budget
(
3
)
.

To better capture realistic scenarios, it is often necessary to model adversarial environments, where components may vary arbitrarily over time. For instance, in autonomous driving, the loss may reflect safety risks such as sudden braking, but it can also increase drastically due to unexpected traffic or hazardous weather. In service robotics, the loss may correspond to task failures or user dissatisfaction, which fluctuate with human preferences or rapidly changing tasks. In such applications, assuming a fixed loss signal is overly restrictive. Therefore, to model these scenarios, it is essential to consider CMDPs under adversarial settings.

Online adversarial CMDPs assume that the loss or cost functions can change arbitrarily across episodes, rather than being drawn from fixed stochastic distributions. Recently, adversarial CMDPs have been investigated under the tabular setting
(
32
;
41
;
39
;
42
;
53
)
. Although these works achieved sublinear regret and constraint violation bounds, they focused only on environments where the state space is finite and relatively small. As a result, their algorithmic guarantees may not extend to settings with large state spaces. Such algorithms are often unsatisfactory in real-world applications, where the number of states is typically extremely large.

To capture settings with a large state space, safe RL with linear function approximation has been studied.
12
proposed a primal-dual policy optimization algorithm for linear mixture CMDPs, where the dynamics are expressed as a mixture of a finite set of basis kernels. For linear CMDPs, assuming linear structure in the loss and cost functions as well as in the dynamics,
15
;
17
designed a primal-dual-type optimistic value iteration algorithm with a softmax policy. For the same setting,
22
developed an algorithm achieving zero constraint violation under the assumption of a known safe policy. Although these algorithms can handle large state spaces, they considered only stochastic settings, where the loss and cost functions are fixed or drawn from underlying distributions. That said, they fail to capture the aforementioned applications, where taking into account adversarial environments is essential when modeling safe RL algorithms.

To overcome these limitations, this paper proposes an algorithm for adversarial linear CMDPs. To handle adversarial losses with constraints, as online primal-dual mirror descent type algorithms become a standard choice, it is natural to consider primal-dual policy optimization for our setting
(
9
;
12
)
. However, when applying primal-dual policy optimization to adversarial linear CMDPs, additional challenges arise—most notably in bounding the covering number of the value function class
(
21
)
.

To elaborate on this challenge, primal-dual policy optimization induces a more intricate policy class, necessitating a new covering number argument. In particular, slightly perturbing the primal variable before optimizing it—namely, the policy in our case—is commonly used in various settings
(
48
;
32
;
12
;
42
)
. The purpose of this step is to derive a compact dual variable bound, which is essential for regret and violation analyses. However, this step breaks the recursive structure of policy optimization, so the resulting policy cannot be represented as a typical softmax policy. As a consequence, the covering number argument becomes non-trivial. In other words, while policy mixing is simple and common, it poses a critical issue for covering number arguments in linear CMDPs. Despite these challenges, we aim to answer the following question:

Can we design a primal-dual policy optimization algorithm for adversarial linear CMDPs that ensures sublinear regret and violation bounds?

Main Contributions

We answer the question affirmatively with
Algorithm

1
, designed for finite-horizon adversarial linear CMDPs, where the losses are adversarially chosen under full-information feedback, and the costs are stochastic under bandit feedback. We summarize our main contributions.

•

We present a primal-dual policy optimization algorithm (
Algorithm

1
) for adversarial linear CMDPs that achieves regret and constraint violation upper bounds of
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28723\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 84054785
, where
\mathchar 29003
is the number of episodes. Our algorithm is the
first
algorithm that achieves sublinear regret and violation in the adversarial linear CMDP setting. Moreover, the algorithm develops a new class of policies, which we refer to as weighted LogSumExp softmax policies, designed to adapt to adversarially chosen loss functions.

•

We establish a covering number argument for the novel class of weighted LogSumExp softmax policies, induced by primal-dual policy optimization algorithms. The main technical difficulty arises from the fact that the weight parameters across policies may differ, preventing direct application of standard properties of the LogSumExp function. Nevertheless, our analysis shows that the covering number under this policy class is bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
, where
\mathchar 29038
is the maximum number of mixing steps, and
\mathchar 29028
is the feature dimension of the linear CMDP.

•

Another challenge in designing a sublinear algorithm for adversarial linear CMDPs lies in the need to simultaneously control both the covering number and the dual variable. To address this, our algorithm incorporates two novel components: (i) periodic policy mixing and (ii) regularized dual updates. Since the covering number grows with the number of mixing steps, the purpose of periodic policy mixing is to regulate the frequency of mixing steps by applying it once in every specified mixing period, rather than in every episode. To incorporate periodic policy mixing, our dual update has to introduce an additional regularization term in order to obtain a compact bound on the dual variable. Together, these algorithmic components allow us to effectively control both the covering number and the dual variable, establishing a sublinear algorithm.

A more detailed review of related work is deferred to the appendix.

3
Challenges and Novel Techniques

Novelty 1: Analysis for Weighted LogSumExp Softmax

We construct a covering number argument with a new policy structure—weighted LogSumExp softmax policies—which arises from combining policy optimization with policy mixing. This policy is given by the weighted sum of exponentials of sums of
\mathchar 29009
-function estimates

2

2

We note that constructing a covering number argument for (

but also on
\mathchar 29009
-function estimates. This means that different policies can have different weight parameters
\{\mathchar 28944_{\mathchar 29033}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29035}
, and thus, well-known properties of LogSumExp cannot be applied. Despite these challenges, our analysis shows that the logarithm of the covering number under (
1
), denoted by
\log{\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}}_{\mathchar 28943}
, grows quadratically with
\mathchar 29038
, where
\mathchar 29038
is the maximum number of mixing steps during the learning process:

\displaystyle\log{\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}}_{\mathchar 28943}\mathchar 12349\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

(2)

Novelty 2: Periodic Policy Mixing

However, deriving an upper bound on the covering number alone is not sufficient to guarantee sublinear regret and violation. In particular, if mixing is applied in every episode, then
\log{\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}}_{\mathchar 28943}
grows to the order of
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
, which is too large to yield a sublinear guarantee. On the other hand, if mixing is performed insufficiently, then the dual variable cannot be bounded, which is critical for violation analysis. These observations highlight an inherent trade-off between the covering number and the size of dual variables, both of which heavily depend on the frequency of mixing. This necessitates a new algorithmic component to balance the two.

The aforementioned trade-off motivates our second contribution—periodic policy mixing—which applies the policy mixing every
\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28994}
episodes
3
3

3

For simplicity, we assume that
\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28994}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28994}
are integers to avoid additional notation such as
\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28994}\right\delimiter 0
.
, where
\mathchar 28994
is a period parameter between 0 and 1. The purpose of the periodic policy mixing is to balance the covering number and the size of dual variables. The covering number can be easily observed from (
2
), since the number of mixing steps is at most
\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28994}
(i.e., the number of episodes divided by the mixing period). However, it remains unclear whether periodic policy mixing is effective in controlling the dual variable. To address this, in the next paragraph, we show that the dual variable can indeed be bounded when periodic policy mixing is combined with a new dual update rule.

Novelty 3: Regularized Dual Update

Our third contribution is another algorithmic component—a new dual update rule with additional regularization. When combined with periodic policy mixing, the dual variable
\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}
is bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28945\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28994}\delimiter 84054785
, where
\mathchar 28945
is the step size and
\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28994}
is the mixing period. For clarity, this bound omits the dependence on
\mathchar 28941\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29000
to highlight the impact of the mixing period.

To elaborate on our dual update method, it takes the following form: given regularization parameters
\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
and the cost value function estimate
\widehat{\mathchar 29014}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29035}
,

\displaystyle\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12832\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28945\delimiter 67273472\widehat{\mathchar 29014}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\underbrace{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785}_{\text{Regularization}}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 314

For interpretation,
\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28945\delimiter 67273472\widehat{\mathchar 29014}_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\delimiter 84054785
corresponds to the standard online gradient ascent step for dual updates in primal-dual algorithms, while the regularization term
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
pulls the dual variable towards
\mathchar 28720
, keeping it compact.
The regularization parameters
\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28722}
will be specified in the following paragraph, along with the intuition for our design.

The intuition behind our regularization is that it serves as a crucial ingredient for a drift-based analysis, a well-known method for bounding dual variables (see, e.g.,
49
;
48
in constrained online convex optimization). To enable drift analysis, these works typically incorporate an inner product term in the dual update, determined by the decision variables and the gradient, i.e.,
\delimiter 69632778\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29044}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 626_{\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 86414091
. In primal-dual policy optimization, we realize that this translates to a term involving the transition kernel, i.e.,
\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}}\delimiter 67482370\delimiter 69632778\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\widehat{\mathchar 29009}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 86414091\delimiter 84267779
. However, since the transition kernel is unknown, this term cannot be directly incorporated into our algorithm. Instead, we take a lower bound on this term, which becomes our regularizing component with the choice of
\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28939\mathchar 28945\mathchar 29000^{\mathchar 28723}
and
\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28939\mathchar 28945\mathchar 29000^{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28946\mathchar 28945\mathchar 29000^{\mathchar 28722}
. In this way, our dual update can be viewed as a key adaptation that enables drift analysis in primal-dual policy optimization for adversarial linear CMDPs.

\mathchar 28955\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29047}\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69645069_{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28675_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035_{\mathchar 29029}}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}}\mathchar 8235\log\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785

Remark 1
.

The feature contraction—originally proposed by
7
for adversarial linear (unconstrained) MDPs—is necessary for the following reason. Specifically, it provides a simpler expression for the policy, which is useful in covering number arguments, via a low-dimensional representation of the sum of
\mathchar 29009
-function estimates. For this, they omitted a clipping operation in the definition of
\mathchar 29009
-function estimates, so the sum collapses into a simple inner product with an optimistic bonus. Instead of clipping, they properly contracted the feature to prevent
\mathchar 29009
-function estimates from expanding uncontrollably. This technique can be replaced with other approaches with the same purpose, such as
36
, but it may lead to higher dependence on
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29000
in regret and violation bounds.

In lines 8-10, the algorithm takes action
\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12824\widehat{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785
for each step
\mathchar 29032\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29000\delimiter 84267779
and observes
\mathchar 28946_{\mathchar 29030\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785
, and
\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 29032\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12824\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785

. Finally, it takes
\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 8235}
to ensure that the dual variable remains nonnegative.

The computational complexity of
Algorithm

1
is
{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 29000\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 69640972{\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}}\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29000\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
, which is independent of
\delimiter 69640972{\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}}\delimiter 69640972
. Specifically, in lines 2-7, computing determinants simply takes
{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 29000\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785
and contracting features takes
{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8705\delimiter 69640972{\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}}\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 8705\mathchar 29000\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785
, since the inverse of the design matrices can be computed in
{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785
by applying the Sherman-Morrison formula. In lines 8-29, the dominant step is function estimation: lines 16-18 take
{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 69640972{\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}}\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29000\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
.

4.1
Comparison of Dual Updates

Since our algorithm is designed for adversarial linear CMDPs, it is worth comparing our dual update with that of algorithms for (i) stochastic linear CMDPs
(
15
)
and (ii) tabular CMDPs with adversarial losses
(
32
)
.

First, we compare with
15
, which focused on stochastic linear CMDPs. The key difference comes from the way the dual variable is regularized. Specifically, while both approaches adopt the standard online gradient ascent procedure, their update rule truncates the dual variable at
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29000\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28941
to ensure that it never exceeds this threshold. In contrast, our update incorporates an extra regularization term to keep the dual variable compact. Although their update is simple and effective in the stochastic setting, it cannot be extended to the adversarial setting, since their analysis relies on the fact that the loss and cost functions are fixed over episodes. This justifies the need for our design of dual update steps in handling adversarial losses.

Second, we compare with
32
, which proposed an occupancy measure-based algorithm for adversarial tabular CMDPs. The main difference in the dual updates arises from the choice of primal variable: policy-based mirror descent versus occupancy measure-based mirror descent. Before elaborating on this, we recall a dual update from the constrained online convex optimization literature, proposed by
48
with minor modifications: given a convex cost function
\mathchar 352\mathchar 12346\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29028}\mathchar 12833\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}
and primal variables
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29028}
,

\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12832\left\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28945\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\mathchar 8235\delimiter 69632778\mathchar 626\mathchar 352\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 86414091\right\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 314

Based on this update, let us show how the dual update for occupancy measure-based algorithms can be derived. Since the occupancy measure serves as the primal variable, we take

denotes an occupancy measure in episode

For simplicity, we assume that

5
Analysis

In this section, we present the proof outline of
Theorem

1
, where the details of the proofs can be found in the appendix. As a first step, we introduce two key ingredients: (i) a high-probability good event and (ii) bounding the dual variable. We note that our covering number argument plays a central role in showing that the good event holds with high probability. Furthermore, to bound the dual variable, the key part is to consider periodic policy mixing and the regularized dual update.

Good Event

We first introduce a high-probability event, denoted by
\mathchar 28997_{\mathchar 29031}
, whose formal definition is provided in the appendix. Basically, the event captures estimation errors for the loss, the cost, and the transition kernel. In addition, it guarantees the boundedness of
\mathchar 29009
-function estimates, reflecting the usefulness of the feature contraction. The following lemma shows that
\mathchar 28997_{\mathchar 29031}
holds with high probability.

Lemma 1
.

Let
\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29047}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29003
. Then for any
\mathchar 28942\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785
,
\Pr\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28997_{\mathchar 29031}\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28942
.

In the proof of
Lemma

1
, the main distinction from previous works arises in our covering number argument. Specifically, our case incorporates weighted LogSumExp softmax policies, induced by mixing the policy. Then we have to derive a Lipschitz property of the policies that have been mixed
\mathchar 29038
times, as the number of mixing steps is a key factor in determining the policy structure. Note that the Lipschitzness of policies is fundamentally required in most covering number arguments.

To attain this property, we prove the following recursion in
\mathchar 29038
, presented here in simplified form:

\delimiter 69645069\widehat{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29027\delimiter 69645069\widehat{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8235\delimiter 69645069{\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}}_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8704{\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathchar 28722}

where
\widehat{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 24891\widehat{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038}
denote the policies that have been mixed
\mathchar 29038
times,
{\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}}_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 24891{\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}}_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 29038}
denote the subsets of the corresponding parameters, and
\mathchar 29027
is a constant. By applying this recursion repeatedly, we can bound the difference between policies using the sum of differences in their parameters, establishing the Lipschitzness. Based on this, we can show that the covering number is bounded as
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
.

7
Conclusion

This paper studies adversarial linear CMDPs, where the losses are adversarially chosen under full-information feedback and the costs are stochastic under bandit feedback. We propose a primal-dual policy optimization algorithm—the first provably efficient algorithm for safe RL with linear function approximation in adversarial settings. We establish a new covering number argument for weighted LogSumExp softmax policies, along with novel algorithmic components that jointly control the covering number and the dual variable. Building on these, we show that the proposed algorithm achieves
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28723\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 84054785
regret and violation bounds. Moreover, our numerical experiments support this. As directions for future work, it remains open to investigate the following challenges: (i) whether
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\sqrt{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 84054785
regret and violation bounds can be achieved in our setting, and (ii) whether a sample-efficient algorithm can be designed for linear CMDPs with adversarial losses under bandit feedback.

Appendix B
Limitations of Prior Work and Naïve Extension

In this section, we clarify why previous works—and their naïve extensions—fail in our setting, where the losses are adversarially chosen in each episode. In particular, we address the following two questions: (i) why the algorithm of
15
fails in the adversarial setting; and (ii) why simply adapting a mirror-descent update is insufficient for adversarial linear CMDPs.

Limitation of Prior Work

While
15
proposed a primal-dual algorithm for linear CMDPs, their analysis is limited to the setting with stochastic losses and constraints. The fundamental reason is that their algorithm is value-based, whose policy is determined solely by the current
\mathchar 29009
-function estimates in each episode; that is
\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29043\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12847\exp\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28939\widehat{\mathchar 29009}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314
Such a policy cannot adapt to time-varying environments, and in particular, cannot handle adversarially chosen losses.

Another limitation is that the dual update of
15
fails to handle adversarial environments. Their key technique is a dual clipping technique—cutting off the dual variable when it exceeds
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29000\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28941
—which enables them to leverage the strong duality of CMDPs. Moreover, to leverage strong duality in their analysis, they reformulate the weighted sum of regret and violation into a simple Lagrangian form (e.g., Appendix D of
15
), i.e., there exists a policy
\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 560}
such that

{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 29003}}\bigg\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29030\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29030\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 8511}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29017\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\delimiter 84054785\bigg\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29030\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 560}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29030\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 8511}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29017\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 560}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

In our case, when losses are adversarially chosen in each episode, reformulating the sum into a simple Lagrangian form is not allowed, i.e.,

{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 29003}}\bigg\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 8511}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29017\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\delimiter 84054785\bigg\delimiter 84054785\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 560}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 8511}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29017\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014_{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953^{\mathchar 560}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

In turn, leveraging strong duality in our setting is non-trivial.

Naïve Extension

To overcome the limitations of value-based algorithms in adversarial settings, a natural approach is to adopt a mirror-descent update, whose regularizer is given by a KL divergence. In our case, this corresponds to a policy optimization, i.e.,

\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\argmin_{\mathchar 28953\mathchar 12850\mathchar 28677}\ \delimiter 69632778\mathchar 28953\mathchar 24891\widehat{\mathchar 29009}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 86414091\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28939}}\mathchar 28996\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28953\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 69640972\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\quad\mathchar 12841\quad\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12847\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\exp\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28939\widehat{\mathchar 29009}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

Due to its recursive formulation, we can see that the resulting policy depends on the sum of all previous
\mathchar 29009
-function estimates, namely
\widehat{\mathchar 28953}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12847\exp\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28939\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\widehat{\mathchar 29009}^{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 84054785
. This is the key difference compared with value-based algorithms.

More technically, let us attempt to adapt the algorithm of
48
to the linear CMDP setting. Their method is a mirror-descent type algorithm—in our case, policy optimization—designed for constrained online convex optimization with adversarial losses and stochastic constraints. Beyond policy optimization, there are two additional distinctions compared with
15
:

•

(Drift Analysis) As previously mentioned, since strong duality is difficult to use in the adversarial setting, the dual clipping technique may not works in the adversarial setting. To address this,
48
came up with a dual update that admits a
Lyapunov drift analysis
. In particular, Lyapunov drift analysis is a standard tool for bounding the dual variable. For this, we first derive an upper bound on the Lyapunov drift term defined as
\mathchar 28673\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28722
, and then utilize it to bound
\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}
. The key point is to make
\mathchar 28673\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785
small enough so that
\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}
stays stable, as
\mathchar 28673\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785
captures the difference between successive dual variables.

•

(Policy Mixing) Another key technique of
48
is
policy mixing
—perturbing the policy before applying the policy optimization update. The motivation behind this technique is to make
\mathchar 28673\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785
small. In particular, a typical bound on
\mathchar 28673\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785
in policy optimization involves KL divergence terms as follows:

\displaystyle\mathchar 28673\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28996\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28953\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 69640972\widetilde{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28996\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28953\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 69640972\widehat{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785

The key issue is that
\mathchar 28996\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28953\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 69640972\widetilde{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785
can become arbitrarily large when
\widetilde{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12825\mathchar 28720
for some
\mathchar 29025\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}
, because the KL divergence is unbounded near the simplex boundary. In contrast, when a mixing step is applied, we ensure that
\widetilde{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28946\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 69640972\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 69640972
for all
\mathchar 29025
, where
\mathchar 28946
denotes the level of mixing. In this case, we can easily show that
\mathchar 28996\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28953\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 69640972\widetilde{\mathchar 28953}_{\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\log\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69640972\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28946\delimiter 84054785
(
Lemma

25
). Hence, the mixing step guarantees that the KL term remains bounded, which in turn prevents the dual variable from blowing up.

Insufficiency of Naïve Extension

While policy mixing is essential to keep the dual variable stable, it becomes problematic in the linear CMDP setting, where the main difficulty arises in the covering number argument. In particular, as described in
Section

3
, we have to derive an upper bound on the covering number of the weighted LogSumExp softmax policy. Establishing such a bound is one of our key challenges and is highly non-trivial. Thus, although adapting
48
to the framework of
15
is a natural step toward handling adversarial losses, obtaining sublinear regret and constraint violation bounds becomes unclear.

Appendix C
Additional Discussions

Intuition on Covering Number

We provide an intuition on why the weighted LogSumExp softmax policy yields
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
, where
\mathchar 29038
denotes the number of mixing steps, while the covering number under greedy or softmax policies is just
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
. Before explaining this, we note that the covering number of a function class depends on (i) how many parameters are required and (ii) how close these parameters must be for the functions to be sufficiently similar. Based on this high-level idea, the number of parameters needed to determine a weighted LogSumExp softmax policy is
{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
, as each
\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034}\widehat{\mathchar 29009}^{\mathchar 29034}
requires
{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
parameters once the feature contraction is applied. Furthermore, we observe that the impact of parameters decreases exponentially as mixing continues, meaning that the parameters must be chosen increasingly close (see the proof of
Lemma

11
). This leads to an additional multiplicative factor
\mathchar 29038
, resulting in
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
.

Choice of Mixing Period

To justify the choice of
\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28723\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}
, we first clarify how
\mathrm{\mathchar 29010\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785
and
\mathrm{\mathchar 29014\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785
depend on the mixing period
\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28994}
. Note that the covering number is closely related to terms (I) and (IV) of the decompositions in (
3
), and the dual variable directly affects term (V). This yields the following simplified regret and constraint violation bounds.

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathchar 29010\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29031\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\sqrt{\mathchar 29003\log{\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}}_{\mathchar 28943}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\quad\mathrm{\mathchar 29014\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\sqrt{\mathchar 29003\log{\mathcal{\mathchar 29006}}_{\mathchar 28943}}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28945\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

We emphasize that
\mathrm{\mathchar 29014\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003\delimiter 84054785
can be bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\sqrt{\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28723\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28994}}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28994}\delimiter 84054785
. This is because the log covering number is bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28722\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28994}\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
, and the dual variable is bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28945\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28994}\delimiter 84054785
. Thus, to minimize the dependency on
\mathchar 29003
, we set
\mathchar 28994\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724
.

Discussion on Lower Bound

We note that the regret lower bound of
\mathchar 28682\delimiter 67273472\sqrt{\mathchar 29000^{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 29028^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 84054785
also applies to our setting, which is for stochastic linear unconstrained MDPs
(
52
;
19
)
. This is because by taking the loss to be fixed across episodes and using a trivial constraint (i.e., taking
\mathchar 29026\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29000
), our problem reduces to a stochastic linear unconstrained MDP. Therefore, we conjecture that there remains room for improving our regret bound by a factor of
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472{\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28721\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}}\delimiter 84054785
.

Additionally, we outline a promising direction toward achieving
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\sqrt{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 84054785
regret and violation in our setting. The main challenge is analyzing the constraint violation without relying on mixing steps. In our current analysis, the mixing step is inevitable to mitigate KL divergence terms in the drift upper bound (
Lemma

17
); these KL divergence terms arise from mirror-descent type updates to control adversarial losses. However, mixing becomes problematic for linear CMDPs because it enlarges the covering number. If one can design an approach that controls the dual variable without mixing, then achieving optimal bounds may become possible.

Appendix D
Related Work

Online Tabular CMDP

Starting from the seminal work of
14
, minimizing regret and constraint violation in online tabular CMDPs has been studied under various settings. Several works
(
26
;
5
;
50
)
considered the case of zero constraint violation under the assumption of a known safe policy. Under the same assumption,
30
studied hard constraint violation—the sum of only positive constraint violations. Without this assumption, the hard constraint violation was studied by
29
;
40
. Moreover,
46
proposed a model-free algorithm for finite-horizon CMDPs, and
45
;
8
proposed algorithms for infinite-horizon average-reward CMDPs. However, these works assume stationary environments. To relax this assumption,
32
studied adversarial losses under full-information feedback. For both adversarial losses and costs,
41
;
42
considered full-information feedback and bandit feedback, respectively. More recently, several papers proposed algorithms for adversarial CMDPs with hard constraint violation guarantees.
39
proposed an algorithm for adversarial losses and stochastic costs under bandit feedback, and
53
studied stochastic losses and adversarial costs under full-information feedback.

Online Linear CMDP

For finite-horizon linear CMDPs,
15
;
17
studied cumulative and hard constraint violations, respectively. Similarly,
16
developed several algorithms for the infinite-horizon average-reward setting.
22
studied achieving zero constraint violation under the assumption of a known safe policy, and
27
studied sample complexity under the assumption of a generative model.
44
studied non-stationary CMDPs, where components of the environment may change subject to bounded total variation. However, this setting differs from adversarial settings, in which the variation of functions is not assumed to be bounded by some factor.
4
;
47
;
33
investigated hard instantaneous constraints, where unsafe actions must not be taken in each step. We also note additional works that are not linear CMDPs but incorporate linear function approximation. There are several works for linear mixture CMDPs with various settings
(
12
;
13
;
38
)
. More generally, the
\mathchar 29041_{\mathchar 28953}
-realizable setting was studied by
43
, which only assumes that value functions can be represented as an inner product of a given feature. However, adversarial environments have not been considered in these settings.

Online Adversarial Linear MDP

Online adversarial linear MDPs have been studied under full-information feedback
(
51
;
36
;
7
)
and bandit feedback
(
31
;
28
;
11
;
37
;
23
;
25
)
. Specifically, in the full-information feedback setting,
51
proposed a multi-batched policy optimization algorithm, achieving a
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28723\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 84054785
regret bound.
36
achieved a
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\sqrt{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 84054785
regret bound, adopting a warm-up phase to obtain a simple expression for policies. In addition,
7
proposed a warm-up free policy optimization algorithm with an improved regret bound. In the bandit feedback setting,
28
introduced the notion of dilated bonus, and
25
proposed two algorithms: one achieved a
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\sqrt{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 84054785
regret bound but was computationally inefficient, and the other achieved
\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28723\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 84054785
and was computationally efficient.

Appendix E
Contracted MDP

In this section, we explain the notion of a contracted MDP
(
7
)
, which is essential for deriving our main results. A contracted MDP is defined by the tuple
\bar{\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29000\mathchar 24891{\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 24891{\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}}\mathchar 24891\{\bar{\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}}_{\mathchar 29032}\}_{\mathchar 29032\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29000}
,
\{\bar{\mathchar 352}_{\mathchar 29032}\}_{\mathchar 29032\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29000}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28954\delimiter 84054785
. Here,
\mathchar 28954\mathchar 12346{\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}}\mathchar 8706{\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}}\mathchar 8706\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29000\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12833\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84267779
specifies the level of contraction. In particular, the loss function and transition kernel are defined as

\displaystyle\bar{\mathchar 352}_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28954\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 352_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}}_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28954\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032\delimiter 84054785\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

Since
\mathchar 28954\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84267779
, it follows that
\bar{\mathchar 352}_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84267779
, meaning that the contraction preserves the boundedness of the original loss function. On the other hand,
\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12850{\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}}}\bar{\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}}_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28721
, which implies that
\bar{\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}}
defines a sub-probability measure. Although this does not satisfy the definition of a probability measure, it is sufficient for our purposes, as the contracted transition kernel is only used in the analysis. Furthermore, it is often called a sub-MDP because its transition kernel is a sub-probability measure.

Accordingly,
\bar{\mathchar 29014}_{\mathchar 352\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 28953}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24635\mathchar 28954\delimiter 84054785
denotes the
\mathchar 28954
-contracted value function with respect to a policy
\mathchar 28953
and a contracted MDP
\bar{\mathcal{\mathchar 29005}}
. Given
\bar{\mathchar 29014}_{\mathchar 352\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29000\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24635\mathchar 28954\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
for all
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12850{\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}}
,
\bar{\mathchar 29014}_{\mathchar 352\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 28953}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24635\mathchar 28954\delimiter 84054785
is defined recursively as

\displaystyle\bar{\mathchar 29014}_{\mathchar 352\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 28953}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24635\mathchar 28954\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}_{\bar{\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28953}\left\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29032}^{\mathchar 29000}\bar{\mathchar 352}_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29043_{\mathchar 29032}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29043\right\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12349\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}_{\mathchar 29025\mathchar 12824\mathchar 28953\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29043\delimiter 84054785}\left\delimiter 67482370\bar{\mathchar 352}_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12850{\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}}}\bar{\mathbb{\mathchar 29008}}_{\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29043\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29025\delimiter 84054785\bar{\mathchar 29014}_{\mathchar 352\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29032\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28953}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 24635\mathchar 28954\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 314

(4)

Appendix G

In this section, we show an upper bound on the covering number of
\widehat{\mathcal{\mathchar 29014}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28995_{\mathchar 28940}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28995_{\mathchar 29047}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28995_{\mathchar 29009}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28995_{\mathchar 29017}\delimiter 84054785

-norm between
\mathchar 29009

Appendix I

\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29017_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28722
.

Lemma 17
.

Assume that the good event
\mathchar 28997_{\mathchar 29031}
holds. For all

The following lemma is to bound the regret due to online mirror descent. Here, the main difference with the standard online mirror descent lemma (e.g.,

;

Lemma 19
.

Let
\mathchar 29000^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29003
. Suppose that
\mathchar 28997_{\mathchar 29031}

\displaystyle\mathchar 12349\widetilde{\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29000^{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28723\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 29000^{\mathchar 28726}\over\mathchar 28941^{\mathchar 28722}}}\mathchar 29003^{\mathchar 28721\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29000^{\mathchar 28725}\over\mathchar 28941}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

Proof.

Consider
\mathchar 29029\mathchar 12850\mathchar 28997

(Proof of the second statement)

Now, we show the second statement. By Hölder’s inequality and the triangle inequality,

By the first statement,

Appendix M
The Use of Large Language Models

Portions of the text were polished using ChatGPT-5, which was employed for grammar checking and sentence refinement.
